36 claims about what LabPlanner does

Generated by bin/c6-report --write on 2026-09-17. Every table and message below came out of a real run made while building this page — nothing is transcribed.

Each numbered sentence is a claim about what the toolkit does at a bench. The evidence for it is underneath. Answer true if that is what should happen, false if a person who knows the chemistry would do it differently.

False is the useful answer. 792 tests pass; every one of them asserts that a number did not change, and not one knows whether pS-A is the right thing to write on a tube. That is the only question this page asks.

The button at the bottom puts your answers in a box to copy. Or just say the numbers.

What goes on a tube

1

When four colonies are picked into tubes, each tube is labelled with the construct name and a clone letter — pS-A, pS-B, pS-C, pS-D — and not a short code.

answered 2026-09-15

The alternative, used until 2026-09-15, was the next letter of a per-experiment sequence (L3h). Two naming schemes on one object.

2

That same name follows the material: the colony and the miniprep made from it both say pS-A, and the sequencing tube adds only the read direction — pS-AF.

answered 2026-09-15

A label that changes at each step has to be cross-referenced at each step. The sequencing tube leaves the building and comes back as a file named for what was written on it.

9

Thirty-two clones asked into one 24-well block is refused rather than seated somehow, and the message names the count and the vessel.

answered 2026-09-15

Which sitting a second block belongs to is a scheduling decision, not a layout one.

$ c6-packet
c6-packet: session 6 (Picking) puts more than 24 clones in one 24-well block. Two blocks is a decision about the session, not about the layout — split the picking across two sittings, or say a larger `vessel=` on the line.

14

A kanamycin transformation is given three control plates beside the real one, and the sheet says what each one would prove.

answered 2026-09-15

A blank plate has four causes; the controls are how you tell them apart.

Controls — three plates, the same for every kan transformation

platewhat goes on itit answers
kan +Mach1 + the kan control plasmidthe cells are competent and took up DNA
kan −Mach1, no DNA addedthe plate is not simply growing untransformed cells
kan streakthe kan control strain, streaked — not transformedanything could have grown on this batch of kan plates

17

A transformation whose antibiotic field cannot be read gets no rescue step and no control plates — and the sheet says nothing about why. The antibiotic column is simply blank, with no note and no STILL TO DECIDE beside it.

needs an answer

The rule behind this says "the sheet carries the question". It does not: it carries an empty cell. Whether cells need an outgrowth before plating turns entirely on which antibiotic it is, so a blank there is the one thing a student cannot work around — and three control plates have silently gone missing too.

An oligo held at 50 µM — neither the working strength nor the stock — is not diluted silently. The sheet says what was actually found and leaves the arithmetic to a person.

needs an answer

A 10 µM working stock is made from a 100 µM one by a known dilution. From 50 µM it is a different sum, and guessing which the tube really is puts the wrong primer concentration in every reaction on the sheet.

needs an answer

26

When the freezer inventory is complete and somebody has said what two letters go on the front of the tubes, the printed workbook asks the student nothing at all. Not one sheet carries a STILL TO DECIDE line, so a person could work the experiment start to finish without stopping to find someone to ask.

needs an answer

Every other example on this page falls short of that by exactly one question, and it is always the same one: the two-letter tube prefix. That cannot be decided by rule, because whether it collides with another group’s tubes is a fact about your lab and not about the chemistry. Answer it and nothing else is left over — which is the claim: the unanswered questions are a finite list that runs out, not a permanent feature of every workbook.

still to decide

  • Nothing. Every question this compiler would not answer by rule has an answer, so no sheet carries a STILL TO DECIDE line.

The order of the work

27

The sessions come out in an order somebody could actually work: stocks and dilutions first, then PCR, then the gel and assembly, then the transformation, and the sequence analysis before anything that uses a verified plasmid.

A construct name too long for a tube cap is a WARNING and the experiment still compiles, because the name came from the file and that is the author’s to choose.

answered 2026-09-15
$ c6-check
note   Construction of pVeryLongConstructName.txt
          LONG_NAME [step 2]
          the file's product is "pVeryLongConstructName", which is 22 characters. That name goes on a 1.5 mL cap with a clone designation after it — "pVeryLongConstructName-A" — and on the sequencing tube after that. 6 or fewer is the aim and 8 is about the limit of what somebody will write by hand. Longer is writeable and less legible, and this is the moment to think about it: after the file is written the name is the key every later step resolves through.

inventory.txt
this is not a freezer, it is a sentence
nor is this

How much of this did anybody look at

Scenarios are unbounded; decisions are not. Nobody can list the experiments a lab will write. But every one of them is compiled by a countable set of decisions, and that set can be listed and watched. So "have we covered every scenario?" has no answer, and "which decisions has nobody looked at?" does.

Two different things, and the second is what this page is for: a run took this path a claim above asserts something about it, and you answered. The suite gives the first. Only you give the second.

in the toolkita run reachesa claim asks about
rules in the rule tables5452
not measurable — see below
design modules1414
8
problem codes1513
8

36 claims drawn from 27 of the runs in the matrix.

The gaps, by name

Six designs no claim asks about
analysis, culture, gel, goldengate, retransform, stock — every one of them runs in the matrix and is asserted by unit tests. Nobody has been shown what they put on a page. This is the largest gap and it is the one worth closing next.
Five reported problems no claim asks about
AMBIGUOUS_ACROSS_FILES, ANALYSIS_VERIFIES_WHAT, DANGLING_PRODUCT, DUPLICATE_INPUT, LEGACY_FORMAT — each fires, and nobody has ruled on whether the sentence it prints is the right sentence.
Two problems nothing reaches
PARSE_FAILED, SESSION_OUTGROWS_VESSEL — no example produces either. SESSION_OUTGROWS_VESSEL became unreachable when allocateWells landed, which is the good outcome; it is named here rather than deleted, because the day it fires again is the day a compile started colliding.
Two rules nothing reaches
label.noSide, labelUniqueness.twoOfAKindOneSitting — held to exactly this set by scenarios.test.js, so a third one appearing fails the suite.

Why "a claim asks about it" is not measurable for rules. One scenario fires about forty rules. A claim pinning one sentence of that run has asserted nothing about the other thirty-nine. An earlier draft of this table counted them all and reported 52 of 54, which looked excellent and only meant the runs behind the claims are ordinary experiments.

And what none of it can see. Two kinds of defect, both of which have already happened here. Correct decisions composing badly — four constructs each picked into well A1, every rule right, the combination wrong, full coverage on both rules throughout. And a decision that should exist and does not: your ruling that "not all these controls are needed when transforming miniprep DNA" was a missing distinction, and no count of what exists will ever find what is absent. That is what reading the claims does and the numbers cannot.
